Logged, not acted on, unless it comes with back discomfort.No supportable number. Included for the baseline, not because the block is expected to move it.
BeightonNot retested. It is a one-time classification (tight / average / loose), not a progress marker.—Score 0–3 is typical; 4 or more out of 9 is the usual cut for generalised joint hypermobility in adults (Beighton 1973; Juul-Kristensen 2017 review). A high score is the one finding that would argue for keeping PAILs/RAILs at the 50–70 % ceilings indefinitely rather than going to 100 % in Block 2.

The "would be a stall" column is diagnostic, not punitive. A stall with a clean log and good sleep is information for Block 2; a stall with a sore joint is the 48-hour rule and nothing else.

05Week 12: the retest protocol

The retest week compares the end of the block to the start of it, using numbers you already keep. It is run at about 60 % of normal set volume so that the measurements are taken fresh and the week doubles as the light week before Block 2. Nothing is maxed; "top set" means the current working weight taken to 1 rep in reserve, exactly the same stopping point as every other set in the programme. The full day-by-day for week 12 is written out in Section 03; this is the summary and the battery procedures.

DayWhat runsWhat is recorded
Mon · Push AWarm-up and daily layer in full. Bench / floor press: 2 ramp sets then 1 top set. Overhead press: 1 top set. Dips, calves, prehab at 2 sets each. Planche primer 2 holds. Splits gentle only.Load × reps on each top set. Compare to the same lift's best set in weeks 1–2 and to the week-2 Brzycki estimate (Section 09).
Tue · Lower + Pull APistol: 1 best clean set per leg at the current tier. Squat variant: 1 top set. RDL: 1 top set. Pull-ups: 1 top set (note added load). Rows, calves, wrist prehab at 2 sets.Load × reps. Pistol tier and reps per leg. For pull-ups, backpack load and reps.
Wed · SkillFull prep. Planche: best clean hold at current tier, 3 attempts, 3 min rest. HSPU: best clean set at current tier, 2 attempts. Bridge: 1 best clean hold. Then the longevity battery and the full-body flexibility battery (below and Section 08). Splits: one gentle 30 s set per position at the lowest support height that keeps the hips square, measure, done.Tier + best hold/reps per ladder. Front split support height both sides; side split hip height. All battery scores.
Thu · Lower + Pull BDeadlift: 2 ramp sets then 1 top set. Split squat: 2 sets per leg. Chin-ups: 1 top set. Rows, calves, leg raises, wrist prehab at 2 sets.Load × reps.
Fri · Push B + tapeMorning, before anything: the eight-measurement tape and Navy body fat. Then a light session: everything at 2 sets. Splits gentle.All eight training-responsive measurements; body fat; arm/calf/neck convergence, arm ÷ 18.0, calf ÷ 22.5.

The five-minute longevity battery

Four tests that the mortality literature in Section 10 actually used or closely resembles. Recorded in week 1 and week 12 and at every block retest. At 23 you should ceiling most of them; the job is to still ceiling them at 53.

  1. Sitting-rising test (SRT). Stand barefoot on the mat. Lower yourself to sitting cross-legged on the floor and stand back up, using as little support as you can. Score 5 for sitting and 5 for rising; subtract 1 for each hand, forearm, knee or side-of-leg touch used for support, and 0.5 for a visible wobble.record: score out of 10 · the published cohorts treat 8 or above as the top band
  2. 10-second one-leg stance. Eyes open, arms by your sides, free foot resting against the back of the standing leg's calf, looking straight ahead. Up to three attempts per side. Pass is 10 s without the free foot touching down.record: pass/fail each side · if both pass, also record best time to 30 s
  3. Standing toe reach. Feet together, knees straight, reach down slowly. Measure fingertip distance to the floor (positive) or past it (negative, hands flat on a block to measure).record: cm, signed
  4. Knee-to-wall ankle dorsiflexion. Half-kneeling or standing lunge facing a wall, test foot flat, drive the knee to the wall without the heel lifting. Slide the foot back until the knee can only just touch. Measure big toe to wall.record: cm each side · asymmetry over 2 cm is worth a note · 10 cm or more is the pistol ankle gate

Reading the block

  • Lifts. A returning lifter's first twelve weeks usually re-acquire old strength quickly; a load increase on every compound and a reps-at-the-same-load increase on the rest is a normal result, not a great one. Compare against your own weeks 1–2 and the week-2 estimates, not against any standard.
  • Ladders. Section 04's "week 12 plausible" column is the benchmark.
  • Tape. Direction only, per the base document.
  • Batteries. Longevity battery should be unchanged at the ceiling. Flexibility battery: Section 04's second table. If anything went backwards, that is a joint-health signal worth more attention than any lift number.

06Warm-up, now with a pulse-raiser

The base document's daily layer is 5–10 minutes of CARs then light band external rotation. That is the specific-joint half of a warm-up and it stays exactly as written. What the base does not have is a general, whole-body half: a few minutes of easy movement that raises heart rate and tissue temperature before the joints are taken through range. Jeff Nippard's programme documents prescribe this as 5–10 minutes of low-intensity work raising heart rate to roughly 100–120 bpm (some of his programmes give 100–135), to the point of a light sweat, followed by 2–3 minutes of foam rolling on tight areas and dynamic drills, then ramp-up sets on the first lift. The heart-rate framing is a reasonable, conservative version of what the warm-up literature generally supports: raise temperature, rehearse the pattern, do not fatigue.

What changes in the daily layer

StepTimeWhatStatus vs base
Pulse-raiser3 minAny easy continuous movement that fits a bedroom: march in place, step-ups on the bench, jump rope if the ceiling allows, shadow-boxing. Target is "breathing a little harder, skin warm," which for most people lands in Nippard's 100–120 bpm band without a monitor. Not a conditioning set.added
CARs5–7 minNeck, shoulders, elbows, wrists, spine, hips, knees, ankles, exactly as the base lists them, now done the FRC way (Section 07): slow, one joint at a time, light whole-body tension, to the painless outer limit of today's range.unchanged · made specific
Cuff wake-up1–2 min2 light sets of band external rotation on Mon/Wed/Fri as written.unchanged
Ramp-up sets2–3 minOn the first main lift: an empty-bar or very light set, then one at roughly half the working load, then work. Already implied by the base; written down here so it is not skipped on a short day.made explicit
Foam rolling—Nippard includes 2–3 minutes on tight areas. The acute evidence shows small range-of-motion gains without a strength penalty, which is real but modest. You do not own a roller and this programme does not require one; if one turns up secondhand, use it on calves and quads before Tuesday/Thursday and treat it as optional.optional

Net cost: about three minutes per session, inside the 45–60 minute envelope. On the two rest days, the base document's gentle daily layer (CARs plus the gentle split touch) continues; the pulse-raiser is for training days only.

07CARs, PNF, PAILs and RAILs: the splits method in full

The base document uses "CARs (controlled articular rotations)" as the daily joint layer and "static holds, PNF contract-relax, light loaded stretching" as the splits method. CARs, PAILs and RAILs come from one place: Functional Range Conditioning (FRC), Dr. Andreo Spina's mobility system (Functional Anatomy Seminars). PNF contract-relax is older and comes from physiotherapy. This section writes all four out as procedures, says what is and is not proven about each, and states the dose the evidence supports.

Evidence status, stated plainly and once

Classical PNF contract-relax has more direct controlled-trial support for pure range-of-motion gain than PAILs/RAILs does. PNF and static stretching both reliably improve range over weeks in randomised trials and the systematic reviews of them (Thomas 2018; Ingram 2024; Page 2012; Behm 2023). PAILs/RAILs, the branded FRC protocol, has never itself been isolated in a controlled trial; a pro-FRC practitioner source acknowledges this directly. That does not mean drop PAILs/RAILs. It means two honest things. First, weeks 1–3, which use the simpler PNF-only method, are if anything the better-evidenced phase of this block, not a warm-up for the real method. Second, PAILs/RAILs afterward is a reasonable, plausible extension of PNF (a long warm-in, a ramped isometric, and an added active pull into the new range), grounded in adjacent work on eccentric and end-range loading and on motor control, rather than a proven-superior upgrade. It is adopted for its procedure and its safety structure. If a future trial shows plain PNF does the same job, nothing in this programme would need to change except the label.

How much, and how often: the dose the evidence supports

  • Weekly accumulated volume beats single-session duration. Thomas et al. 2018 (systematic review of chronic stretching) found that total weekly time per muscle predicted range-of-motion gains, with a threshold around 5 minutes per week; time within a single session did not matter once the weekly total was met; and frequency was positively associated with gains, with the authors suggesting at least 5 days a week.
  • Per set, 15–30 seconds is enough; beyond 2–4 reps per position adds nothing measurable. Bandy & Irion 1997 (hamstring static stretching, 6 weeks, 5 days/week) found 30 seconds per rep as effective as 60, and one rep per day as effective as three. Page 2012 (Current Concepts in Muscle Stretching, IJSPT) reviews the same literature and lands on 15–30 s holds, 2–4 reps, for the general population. Ingram 2024 adds a plateau around 4 minutes per muscle group per session and 10 minutes per week.
  • What this means here. The gentle daily touch, written out below, is the evidence-backed floor and the thing that should never be skipped. On its own (2–4 reps of 20–30 s per position, every day) it clears the Thomas threshold and the Ingram plateau for every split position. The three intense sessions are for a different goal, a full split, which is an extreme end-range outcome the general stretching literature was not designed to test; the intense-session cap of three non-consecutive days is a conservative coaching heuristic, not a trial finding. If you ever had to keep only one, keep the daily touch.

Procedure 1: CARs, done the FRC way (every day, every session)

  1. Isolate one joint. Everything else stays still; the rotation comes from the joint being trained, not from the spine or the neighbouring joint cheating range in.one joint at a time, both sides where paired
  2. Create light whole-body tension (irradiation). Brace the trunk, make loose fists, keep breathing. Around 20–30 % effort in the daily layer: enough to feel the joint doing the work, not a strain.irradiation 20–30 % · never hold the breath
  3. Move slowly to the painless outer limit. One full rotation takes 15–30 seconds. The goal is the largest circle today's joint can draw under control; when a spot catches or pinches, back off a little and keep the circle smooth.3–6 slow rotations per joint per direction
  4. Order and time. Neck, shoulders, elbows, wrists, spine (segmental if you can), hips, knees, ankles. At 3 reps per direction this is about 6 minutes; at 5 reps closer to 10. On Wednesday, wrist CARs get extra reps.5–10 min, fits the base layer exactly

Procedure 2: the gentle daily touch (every day, including rest days and deload)

This is a dose, not a session. The point is to accumulate minutes across the week, so it is done lightly enough that there is no reason to skip it tomorrow.

  1. Front split, each leg. Kneel in a lunge, back knee down on the mat, hands at your logged support height (blocks / fingers / none). Slide the front heel forward until the back of the front thigh and the front of the rear hip register a mild stretch you could hold while talking, about 4 out of 10. Hips square: both hip bones facing straight ahead. Breathe in for four, out for four.hold 20–30 s · come up · 2–3 reps · then the other leg · 3–5 min total
  2. Side split. Stand feet wide, toes forward or slightly out, hands on the floor in front (or on the bench if the floor is out of reach). Walk the feet apart until the inner thighs register a mild 4-out-of-10 stretch, knees straight but not locked, kneecaps pointing up. Breathe four in, four out.hold 20–30 s · walk in to rest · 2–4 reps · 3–5 min total
  3. What is deliberately absent. No contract phase, no sinking on the exhale, no counting down to a deeper position. If it feels like work, it is too much for the daily touch.stretch in the muscle belly only · knee / hip-socket / sitting-bone pain = 48-hour event

Procedure 3: static hold + PNF contract-relax (Mon · Wed · Fri in weeks 1–3; Mon in weeks 4–6 and 8–11; Fri in weeks 4–6)

Positions, in this order every session: front split left leg forward, front split right leg forward, side split. Before the first hold, write today's hand-support height in the log for each position (blocks / fingers / none; for the side split, hip height from the floor in hand-widths). This is a measurement of where you are, not an assumption about flexibility you may or may not have.

  1. Settle in and hold. At the logged support height, to a firm but tolerable stretch in the muscle belly, about 6–7 out of 10. Front split: back knee down then straightened, front heel sliding forward, hips square, hands on the support. Side split: feet wide, kneecaps up, hands on the floor or bench in front. Breathe in for four, out for four.30–60 s
  2. Contract. Without moving, press the stretched leg hard into the floor. Front split: the front heel drags back toward you and the back knee presses down. Side split: both legs press down and squeeze inward as if closing the split. Nothing actually moves: you are contracting the stretched muscles against the floor, building to a hard but not maximal effort (about 7 out of 10), breathing throughout.5–10 s
  3. Relax and sink. Let go completely on a long exhale. For the next 5–10 seconds the stretch eases; sink slightly deeper in that window, a centimetre or two, never a lunge.relaxed window 5–10 s
  4. Hold the new depth. 30–60 s. That completes one cycle. Repeat steps 2–4.2–4 cycles per position · Wednesday runs the full count, Mon/Fri run 2
  5. Progress by support height only. When a position can be held at the current support for the full first hold with hips square and the stretch under 6/10, take the next lower support (blocks → fingers → none) next session. The log tracks support height, not how it felt.Mon/Fri 10–12 min · Wed 12–20 min

Procedure 4: PAILs and RAILs (Wed from week 4 at ≤ 50 %; Wed + Fri in weeks 8–11 at ≤ 70 %)

PAILs (progressive angular isometric loading) is a ramped contraction of the muscle being stretched, pushing against the floor in the stretch position. RAILs (regressive angular isometric loading) immediately follows: a contraction of the opposite side of the joint, actively pulling deeper into the range. The sequence is PNF contract-relax with a long warm-in, a slow ramp, and an added active pull. Same three positions, same support-height log first.

  1. Passive hold. Settle into the position at the deepest depth you can hold with the hips square and no joint pain. Breathe in for four, out for four. This is the warm-in and should feel like letting go, not pushing.2 min minimum per position
  2. PAILs ramp. Tense the whole body (fists, trunk; jaw loose, still breathing), then drive the stretched leg into the floor (front split: front heel and back knee press down; side split: both legs press down and inward), ramping from about 10 % to the day's ceiling over 10–20 seconds. Hold the ceiling 5–15 seconds. The range does not change during PAILs; only the tension does.ramp 10–20 s · hold 5–15 s · ceiling per the schedule
  3. RAILs, no release between. Switch smoothly to contracting the opposing muscles to pull yourself deeper (front split: pull the front heel back toward you and the back knee forward through the floor; side split: pull the legs apart). Then exhale and settle into whatever new depth appeared.5–15 s · smooth transition, not a reset
  4. Rounds. One to three rounds per position, about 3–5 minutes each including the passive hold. Weeks 4–6: 1–2 rounds; weeks 8–11: 2 rounds on Wednesday, 1–2 on Friday.1–3 rounds per position
WeeksWherePAILs/RAILs effort ceilingEverything else
1–3Nowhere yet—Static holds + PNF contract-relax, Mon/Wed/Fri. The better-evidenced method; not a placeholder.
4–6Wednesday only≤ 50 %Mon and Fri stay PNF. Learning the ramp and the switch is the point; depth is not.
7Nowhere—Deload: gentle daily touch only.
8–11Wednesday + Friday≤ 70 %Monday stays PNF contract-relax. Still three intense sessions, non-consecutive.
12Nowhere—Retest week: gentle touch, measure support height.
Block 2+Wed + Friup to 100 %Only once weeks 4–11 produced zero 48-hour events in the hips or knees, and only if the Beighton screen (Section 08) did not come back high. Maximal PAILs/RAILs are strength work and recover like strength work.
Safety, in order of strictness

FRC practitioners treat soreness lasting past 24–36 hours as the signal to reduce PAILs/RAILs intensity or frequency. The base document's rule is stricter about where and governs here: stretch sensation belongs in the muscle belly, and any pain at the knee, the inside of the hip joint or the sitting-bone tendon attachment is a 48-hour event that drops that position out for the week. The two rules stack; the stricter one wins at every point.

Background reading: Kurz, Stretching Scientifically

Thomas Kurz's Stretching Scientifically (Stadion, 4th ed. 2003) is the most commonly cited science-grounded flexibility book in the splits and martial-arts communities, and it is a legitimate recommendation for the why: it explains the physiology of the stretch reflex, why the contract-relax method works, why strength through the range matters, and why morning static stretching and evening relaxed stretching behave differently. Two honest caveats. It is dated: much of its source material is Soviet-era sports-science literature, some of which has not been replicated in the modern trial literature above. And its own programming is thin and vague on practical day-to-day instruction, which is exactly the problem this page is fixing. So: read it for grounding theory, not as a competing protocol to switch to. The method here stays FRC-structured on top of PNF; Kurz is the "why this works" reference, not a replacement system.

Kelly Starrett, for completeness

Kelly Starrett (The Ready State; Built to Move, 2023) frames mobility as ten self-tests with a benchmark and a daily 10-minute practice attached. Two of those tests (the floor sit-and-rise, the balance stand) are the Araújo-group mortality predictors in Section 10, which is why they are in the week-12 battery. Nothing else from his system is imported.

08Full-body flexibility baseline: five home tests and one screen

You pointed out that the flexibility work covered the two splits and nothing else. This section does not add stretching to the week; it adds measurement, so that the shoulders, hip flexors, thoracic spine and hamstrings each have a number at week 1 and week 12, and so that anything going backwards is visible. Equipment: a tape measure or yardstick, a wall, and the bench or the edge of the bed. Run all of it on week-1 Wednesday after the ladder placements, about ten minutes, and again on week-12 Wednesday. Write every number down; the point is the comparison, not the score.

Flexitest and the Flexindex: the real full-body number, and why you cannot run it at home

The flexibility-mortality cohort in Section 10 (Araújo 2024) used the Flexitest: twenty passive joint movements, each scored 0–4 by a trained assessor against reference drawings, summed into a single Flexindex out of 80. It is the one well-validated composite full-body flexibility number, and it is worth knowing it exists. It is not home-administrable: the scoring depends on an assessor moving each joint passively to its limit and matching the position to the standard plates, and the published norms assume exactly that. The five tests below are the practical substitute. They are not a Flexindex and should not be compared to the published Flexindex numbers; they are your own week-1 numbers, to be compared only with your own week-12 ones.

Test 1: Apley's scratch test (shoulder: overhead reach and behind-the-back reach)

  1. Set up. Stand tall, arms relaxed. Mark the top of each shoulder blade (the bony ridge you can feel) with a finger so you know the landmark.no equipment beyond the tape
  2. Movement. Reach the right hand over the right shoulder and down the back as far as it goes, palm against the back, fingers pointing down. At the same time reach the left hand behind the back from below, palm facing out, fingers pointing up, as far as it goes. Do not force; stop at the comfortable end.hold 2–3 s
  3. Measure. Have the tape ready (or photograph in a mirror): measure the gap between the two middle fingertips in cm. If the fingers overlap, record the overlap as a negative number. If they cannot both reach the midline, record the gap honestly even if it is 20 cm.record: cm, signed, "right over"
  4. Swap. Left hand over the top, right hand from below. Record "left over." The side-to-side difference is as useful as either number.record: cm, signed, "left over"

Test 2: modified Thomas test (hip flexor length, each leg)

  1. Set up. Sit on the very edge of the bed or the flat bench so the sitting bones are at the edge. Pull one knee to the chest with both hands, and holding it, roll back to lie flat, letting the other leg hang off the edge. The low back must stay flat against the surface: if it arches, pull the held knee in a little more.bench or bed edge · phone on a stand for a side photo helps
  2. Read the hanging leg. Look (or photograph from the side) at two things. (a) Is the hanging thigh level with the surface, or does it lift toward the ceiling? A thigh that rises above level means the hip flexors (iliopsoas) are short. (b) Does the hanging knee stay bent to roughly 90° with the shin hanging straight down, or does it straighten out in front? A knee that drifts toward straight means the rectus femoris (front of thigh, crosses the knee) is short.record: thigh level / lifted by about __ cm at the knee; knee ~90° / straightened to about __°
  3. Also note. Whether the hanging thigh drifts out to the side (lateral thigh tightness) and whether the held side's low back is arching. Then swap legs.each leg · a side photo makes week 12 comparable

Test 3: seated trunk rotation (thoracic mobility)

  1. Set up. Sit on the bench or a chair with feet flat and knees together, squeezing a rolled towel or a book between the knees so the hips cannot turn. Hold the barbell, a broom handle or a dowel across the front of the shoulders with both hands, or cross the arms with the hands on the opposite shoulders.hips locked by the knees · spine tall
  2. Movement. Rotate the torso to the right as far as it goes without the knees separating or the hips turning, keeping the stick level and the spine tall (no leaning back). Hold two seconds.no momentum
  3. Measure. Estimate the angle the stick has turned from straight-ahead: 45° is halfway to sideways, 90° would be the stick pointing straight ahead and back. A phone photograph from directly above (ceiling view, phone on a shelf or held by someone) gives a readable angle against the floorboards or mat edge. Without a photo, note where the ends of the stick point relative to the room (e.g. "about 50°, slightly past the bedpost"). Repeat left.record: degrees, each direction · normal range is about 45–60° per side

Test 4: sit-and-reach (hamstrings and low back)

  1. Set up. Barefoot, sit on the mat with the legs straight, feet together, soles flat against the wall or against the side of the flat bench. Lay the tape or yardstick along the floor between the legs with a mark at the point where the heels / soles are: that mark is zero.warm first: do the test after CARs, not cold
  2. Movement. Stack one hand on top of the other, fingertips level, and reach forward slowly along the tape as far as possible, knees staying straight (pressed down), exhaling. Hold the furthest point for two seconds; no bouncing.3 attempts · record the best
  3. Measure. Distance of the fingertips past the zero mark (positive) or short of it (negative), in cm.record: cm, signed

Test 5: standing toe reach and knee-to-wall

Already in the longevity battery (Section 05); counted here so the full-body picture is in one place. Toe reach: hamstrings and posterior chain, standing. Knee-to-wall: ankle dorsiflexion each side, and the pistol ankle gate.

One-time screen: Beighton hypermobility score (week 1 only, not tracked)

Nine points, no equipment. Run once at week 1. It is not a progress marker and is not retested; it classifies you as tight, average or unusually loose, which decides how aggressively PAILs/RAILs should ever be pushed. Score each item 1 if yes, 0 if no.

  1. Thumb to forearm (1 point each side). Palm down, use the other hand to bend the thumb back toward the inside of the forearm. Point if the thumb touches the forearm.left · right
  2. Little finger past 90° (1 point each side). Palm flat on the bench, use the other hand to bend the little finger back. Point if it goes past 90° to the back of the hand.left · right
  3. Elbow hyperextension (1 point each side). Arm straight out, palm up, lock the elbow. Point if the forearm bends back past straight by about 10° or more (a visible backward bend at the elbow).left · right
  4. Knee hyperextension (1 point each side). Standing, lock the knees. Point if the knee bends backward past straight by about 10° or more (seen from the side, the leg bows backward).left · right
  5. Palms flat on the floor (1 point). Feet together, knees straight, bend forward. Point if both palms rest flat on the floor.total: 0–9
Reading it

0–3 is typical for an adult man and means the splits are limited by muscle length and the nervous system's tolerance for stretch: PAILs/RAILs can progress on the block schedule. 4 or more is the usual adult cut for generalised joint hypermobility (Beighton 1973; the 2017 international consensus reviewed by Juul-Kristensen uses 5 for adults under 50, 4 at 50 and over). A high score means the joints are already loose and the limiting factor is control, not length: in that case the daily touch and the CARs stay, the PNF phases stay, but the PAILs/RAILs ceiling stays at 50–70 % permanently, Block 2's "up to 100 %" is not taken, and any end-range pain is treated as a joint event even without the 48-hour wait. The score itself does not change training in weeks 1–3.

A future consideration, named and not added: Zhan Zhuang

Zhan Zhuang ("standing like a post"), the standing-posture practice from the Chinese internal arts, was looked at for this block because it targets two things the programme does not touch: sustained trunk and postural endurance held in stillness, and the autonomic (relaxation, breathing-pattern) effects practitioners report. It is not built into the weekly structure, because it was not approved for inclusion and because the supporting evidence is thin and mostly secondary-sourced (practitioner accounts and general qigong reviews, not trials of standing practice itself). The horse stance specifically, the loaded wide-legged standing hold, was researched and found redundant with the pistol-squat ladder, which already provides deep-knee, long-hold leg work with better carryover, so it is not being added. If you want to try Zhan Zhuang, it would be a 5–10 minute standalone standing practice on rest days, outside the training clock, and nothing else on this page would change.

09Strength baseline without a 1RM: what exactly it is

You asked what the non-1RM strength baseline should actually be. The answer: no one-rep-max testing, ever, in this block or any later one. A true 1RM attempt is the single most joint-stressful thing a lifter can do, and it buys nothing this programme needs; double progression works on reps at a working weight, not on a percentage of a max. This is consistent with Priority 1 and is not up for revision.

The estimated-max set, week 2

In week 2, alongside the one-time strict max-rep tests for push-ups, pull-ups and dips (mobility companion, Section 05; also written into the week-2 cards in Section 03), each main barbell lift gets one estimated-max set. It replaces the first working set of that lift that day, so no session gets longer.

  1. Which lifts. Bench / floor press and overhead press (Monday); squat variant and Romanian deadlift (Tuesday); deadlift (Thursday). Pull-ups and rows are not included: pull-ups already get the strict rep test, and the row's form drifts too much under heavy load for a clean estimate.5 lifts, one set each
  2. Pick the weight. After the normal ramp-up sets, load a weight heavier than the current working weight: one you expect to lift about 5 times. Err on the lighter side; the formula is more accurate at low reps but the set must still stop short of failure.target 3–8 reps
  3. Perform the set. Same technique standard as every other set in the programme: last rep looks like the first. Stop with 2–4 reps clearly in reserve. Not to failure, not to the first ugly rep; stop when you are sure you had two more.2–4 RIR
  4. Compute. Brzycki formula: estimated 1RM = weight × (36 ÷ (37 − reps)). Example: 60 kg for 6 reps → 60 × (36 ÷ 31) = 60 × 1.161 ≈ 69.7 kg. Because you stopped with reps in reserve, the real max is higher than this number; that is fine, the number is a consistent floor to compare against at the next week-2.write the estimate; never attempt it
  5. Finish the session. The remaining sets of that lift run at the normal working weight and the normal rep range.no other line changes
What the number is and is not

The Brzycki estimate is reliable only in roughly the 3–10 rep range; the formula's error grows quickly at higher rep counts (at 12+ reps it is routinely off by 10 % or more, in either direction, because high-rep sets measure endurance as much as strength). That is why the set is loaded to land at 3–8. The estimate is never attempted directly: its whole purpose is to give a comparable strength number across blocks without ever loading a joint to its limit. The comparison that matters is week 2 of Block 1 against week 2 of Block 2, same lift, same method; the week-12 top sets (taken to 1 rep in reserve at the working weight) are a second, independent read.

Why this and not a 3RM or 5RM

A 3RM or 5RM "to failure" is a max effort in all but name and carries most of the same joint risk; the last rep of a true 5RM is where form breaks. Stopping at 2–4 reps in reserve keeps the set inside the same technique standard as the rest of the programme while still producing a number. The price is a slightly conservative estimate. That is the right direction to be wrong in.

10Flexibility, movement and longevity: the evidence

You asked for the actual studies. Here they are, with the numbers as published, followed by what they do and do not imply for a 23-year-old.

Flexibility and death: the Flexitest cohort

The study you will have seen referenced is Araújo et al., 2024, Scandinavian Journal of Medicine & Science in Sports: a prospective cohort of 3,139 adults aged 46–65 (2,087 men, 1,052 women) assessed at the CLINIMEX exercise-medicine clinic in Rio de Janeiro with the Flexitest, a 20-movement passive range-of-motion battery scored 0–4 per movement and summed into a "Flexindex" (0–80). Over a mean 12.9 years of follow-up there were 302 deaths (9.6 %). Survivors' Flexindex was about 10 % higher than non-survivors' in both sexes. Comparing the least flexible to the most flexible, after adjusting for age, body-mass index and health status, mortality risk was 1.87 times higher in men and 4.78 times higher in women. Women scored about 35 % higher on the Flexindex than men overall.

The same clinic's other "non-aerobic fitness" predictors

TestStudyCohortFinding
Sitting-rising test (sit to the floor and stand, scored 0–10 by supports used)Brito, Ricardo, Araújo et al., 2014, Eur J Prev Cardiol2,002 adults aged 51–80, median 6.3 yr follow-up, 159 deathsEach one-point increase in score associated with a 21 % lower mortality; scores of 0–3 carried roughly 5–6 times the mortality of scores 8–10. A 2025 follow-up from the same group (Eur J Prev Cardiol) extended the result to natural and cardiovascular deaths in a larger cohort.
10-second one-leg stanceAraújo et al., 2022, Br J Sports Med1,702 adults aged 51–75, median 7 yr follow-upInability to balance 10 s on one leg: adjusted hazard ratio 1.84 for all-cause death. About 20 % of the cohort failed; the fail rate rose steeply with age (around half at 70, near 90 % at 81–85).
Muscular strength (grip and knee extension)García-Hermoso et al., 2018, Arch Phys Med Rehabil, meta-analysis of about 2 million peopleApparently healthy adults, many cohortsHigher grip strength: 31 % lower all-cause mortality. Higher knee-extension strength: 14 % lower. Not a flexibility finding, included because the "movement quality for longevity" case rests on strength, flexibility and balance together, and the lifting half of this programme is the strength input.
What these studies do not show

Every result above is observational. None of them is a trial in which people were made more flexible or stronger and then lived longer. Low flexibility, poor floor-rising and poor balance in a 60-year-old are plausibly markers of the same things that kill people (sarcopenia, frailty, inactivity, neurological decline), not independent causes. The authors of the 2024 paper say as much and call for flexibility to be assessed routinely; they do not claim stretching extends life. Also, every cohort is middle-aged or older. There is no cohort of 23-year-olds, because almost no 23-year-old fails these tests, and almost none die during follow-up.

What it means at 23

Three things, none of which require changing the programme and all of which argue for keeping its priorities.

  • The markers are a floor to protect, not a target to chase. You should score 10 on the sitting-rising test and pass the balance test on both legs now. The useful fact is that half of 70-year-olds cannot; the decline between here and there is the thing to prevent, and the earliest possible baseline (week 1) is how you will know if it starts.
  • The programme already trains the mechanism behind every marker. Floor-rising is hip and ankle range plus relative leg strength: the splits, the squat pattern and CARs. One-leg balance is ankle control and hip stability: single-leg RDLs, Bulgarian split squats, ankle CARs. Grip and knee-extension strength: deadlifts, pull-ups, squats. Nothing new is needed; the longevity case is a second reason for what the base document already prescribes.
  • Joint health as Priority 1 is the longevity position. A programme that trades a wrist or a hip for a faster planche is also trading the 50-year version of these tests. The base document's override is, read this way, a longevity rule.

How much stretching, revisited for the longevity angle

The dosing literature the base splits section drew on is consistent and worth stating precisely, because it bears on the "gentle daily touch" line more than on the intense sessions.

  • Thomas et al., 2018 (Int J Sports Med, systematic review of chronic stretching): total weekly time per muscle was what predicted range-of-motion gains, with a threshold around 5 minutes per week; time within a single session did not matter once the weekly total was met; weekly frequency was positively associated with gains, with the authors suggesting at least 5 days a week; and static stretching produced larger long-term gains than ballistic or PNF.
  • Ingram et al., 2024 (Sports Medicine, 189 studies, 6,654 adults, mean age about 27): static stretching has a moderate acute effect (g = 0.63) and a large chronic effect (g = 0.96) on flexibility. Gains plateaued at about 4 minutes per muscle group per session and about 10 minutes per muscle group per week, with no extra benefit beyond that. Stretch intensity, session frequency, intervention length, age, sex and training status did not moderate the effect; people with poor baseline flexibility gained more.
  • Behm et al., 2023 (Sports Medicine – Open, 47 studies): a single bout of stretching of any type produces a small acute range-of-motion increase; the type matters less than doing it.
  • Warneke et al., 2024 (Sports Medicine – Open): long-duration static stretching produces small but real increases in maximal strength (d ≈ 0.30) and muscle size (d ≈ 0.20) in the stretched muscle, and only when the dose is long and frequent. Relevant to the splits, not a reason to stretch instead of lift.

What this means for the programme. The 3–5 minute gentle daily touch per split position already clears both the Thomas threshold (5 min/week) and the Ingram plateau (10 min/week) on its own, seven days a week, which is the high-frequency pattern Thomas found most effective. That is the longevity-relevant dose: a modest amount, every day, forever. The three intense sessions on top of it are for a different goal, a full split, which is an extreme end-range outcome the general stretching literature was not designed to test; Ingram's "no benefit beyond 10 minutes" is a finding about average flexibility gains in average people, not a ceiling on what full splits require. The base document's framing stands exactly: the intense-session cap is a conservative coaching heuristic, the daily touch is the evidence-backed floor, and if you ever had to keep only one of the two, keep the daily touch.

11Nippard, frequency and the age mismatch

Jeff Nippard is a natural bodybuilder whose programmes and videos cite the exercise-science literature, and two of his positions were raised for this block. One transfers cleanly (the warm-up, Section 06). The other needs to be handled carefully because it was built for a different person.

What could and could not be verified

  • Warm-up: verified from his own programme documents. 5–10 minutes raising heart rate to about 100–120 bpm (100–135 in some programmes), a light sweat, optional 2–3 minutes of foam rolling on tight areas, dynamic drills, ramp-up sets. Adopted in Section 06.
  • Lower-frequency "longevity" split for natural lifters over 30, with a "96-hour recovery curve": only partly verifiable. His Essentials programme (jeffnippard.com) does offer a 3-day option and argues for lower volume at higher intensity for time-constrained lifters, and his public commentary on frequency is consistent with "fewer days is fine if volume is kept." But the specific "30+, 3 days a week, 96-hour recovery curve" phrasing turned up only on third-party summary sites, not in a primary Nippard video or document that could be checked. It is treated below as a plausible paraphrase of his position, not a quotation.
  • "Science Based Movers": no channel of that exact name could be found. The real, checkable lineage of the "science-based movement" coaching world used here is Spina's FRC (Section 07) and Starrett's The Ready State. If you had a specific creator in mind, send the link and this section gets corrected.

The age mismatch, addressed directly

You are 23, turning 24. A guideline written for natural lifters over 30, aimed at protecting joints across decades by training each pattern less often, does not transfer to you as a frequency rule, and importing it would quietly contradict the base document's five-day structure, which was chosen for other, good reasons. Three points make the decision clear:

  • The evidence on frequency does not favour three days for your situation. Once weekly volume is matched, the hypertrophy difference between training a muscle twice versus three or more times a week is small (Pelland et al., 2024 meta-regression; the earlier Schoenfeld frequency reviews point the same way). Five sessions of 45–60 minutes with every pattern twice a week is simply the way the base document fits 10–20 sets per muscle into the available time. Three longer sessions would hit the same volume with more fatigue per session, which is worse for skill practice and no better for joints.
  • The underlying principle is already in the base document, applied per joint rather than per week. The reason to lower frequency in an older lifter is connective-tissue recovery. The base handles that directly: pressing shoulder and extended wrist are loaded on three non-adjacent days with at least 48 hours between heavy exposures, pull-and-lower days sit in between, hard splits are capped at three non-consecutive sessions, and the 48-hour rule catches anything that slips through. That is a joint-recovery design; it does not need to be a lower-frequency one.
  • The transferable part is the attitude, and it is adopted. Nippard's longevity argument is that natural lifters should plan for decades, not months, and keep set quality high rather than piling on volume. The base document's "repeat, don't rotate," its volume at the lower-middle of the evidence range for the lower body, its 1–3 reps shy of failure, and this block's early deload are the same attitude in a 23-year-old's calendar. If, at 30-plus, a joint starts needing more than 48 hours, the right move is the one the base already describes (drop a Friday Push B, not a pull day) and at that point a four-day version of the same week becomes the standing structure. Not now.

12The aesthetic direction, briefly

You shared a post from a natural bodybuilder (@shotta_john, "DonJohn") captioned "Discipline is boring until the results start speaking," as a physique you like: visibly muscular, athletic and defined, the result of ordinary consistent training rather than anything exotic. That is useful context and it is all it is. The base document already settled, after an earlier correction, that this programme does not lock onto any individual's physique as a target, including Reeves, whose ratios sit in Section 04 of the base as a reference point for reading your own numbers and nothing more. The same applies here: the post confirms the direction ("visibly muscular and defined through real training") and changes no number, no exercise, no rule. The caption, as it happens, is a fair one-line summary of a 12-week block in which nothing changes except the log.

13After week 12

The honest answer, and the one consistent with the base document's "repeat, don't rotate" principle: re-measure everything, start Block 2 with the updated baselines, same five-day week.

  • Block 2 week 1 begins the Monday after the retest, at the week-11 loads and reps (not the retest top sets, which were single sets taken fresh). It is a loading week, not a placement week; placement was a Block 1 feature.
  • Same structure, same ladders, same rules. Mon Push A, Tue Lower + Pull A, Wed Skill, Thu Lower + Pull B, Fri Push B. Deload at week 7, retest at week 12. The only structural edits allowed between blocks are the ones the base document already names as responses to specific findings: a fifth squat set if the squat stalled, two sets of curls on Thursday if the arm has not moved on three consecutive tapes, moving a lift one column left on the substitution ladder when equipment arrives.
  • Updated baselines. The week-12 tape becomes the "now" column for the base document's ratio reads. The arm = calf = neck convergence point is re-set at the new neck measurement, as the base specifies. The week-12 battery scores become the floor to protect for the next block.
  • PAILs/RAILs may go to full effort on Wednesday and Friday in Block 2 only if Block 1 produced zero hip or knee 48-hour events in weeks 4–11. Otherwise they stay at 70 % for another block. A Beighton score of 4 or more (Section 08) keeps them at 70 % permanently.
  • What would justify a bigger change. Only a Priority 1 event: a recurring ache in the same joint across two blocks despite the 48-hour rule. That is a signal to remove a movement, not to redesign the week. Variety-seeking is not a reason, by your own instruction.

Block 2 is therefore not a new document. It is this one with the dates moved forward twelve weeks and the week-1 numbers replaced by the week-12 ones.

14Sources

Sources already listed in the base document (Reeves, Barban, the Navy method, ANSUR 1988, Steven Low, Plotkin 2022, Schoenfeld 2017) are not repeated. Everything below was checked for this block.

  • Araújo CGS, et al. Reduced Body Flexibility Is Associated With Poor Survival in Middle-Aged Men and Women: A Prospective Cohort Study. Scand J Med Sci Sports. 2024;34:e14708. (3,139 adults aged 46–65; 12.9-year follow-up; 302 deaths; low-vs-high Flexindex mortality 1.87× men, 4.78× women.)
  • Brito LBB, Ricardo DR, Araújo DSMS, Ramos PS, Myers J, Araújo CGS. Ability to sit and rise from the floor as a predictor of all-cause mortality. Eur J Prev Cardiol. 2014;21(7):892–898. Follow-up: Araújo CGS et al. Sitting–rising test scores predict natural and cardiovascular causes of deaths in middle-aged and older men and women. Eur J Prev Cardiol. 2025; doi:10.1093/eurjpc/zwaf325.
  • Araújo CG, de Souza e Silva CG, Laukkanen JA, et al. Successful 10-second one-legged stance performance predicts survival in middle-aged and older individuals. Br J Sports Med. 2022;56:975–980. doi:10.1136/bjsports-2021-105360.
  • García-Hermoso A, Cavero-Redondo I, et al. Muscular Strength as a Predictor of All-Cause Mortality in an Apparently Healthy Population: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Data From Approximately 2 Million Men and Women. Arch Phys Med Rehabil. 2018;99(10):2100–2113.e5.
  • Thomas E, Bianco A, Paoli A, Palma A. The Relation Between Stretching Typology and Stretching Duration: The Effects on Range of Motion. Int J Sports Med. 2018;39(4):243–254.
  • Ingram LA, Tomkinson GR, et al. Optimising the Dose of Static Stretching to Improve Flexibility: A Systematic Review, Meta-analysis and Multivariate Meta-regression. Sports Med. 2024; doi:10.1007/s40279-024-02143-9. (189 studies, 6,654 adults; plateau at ~4 min/session, ~10 min/week per muscle group.)
  • Behm DG, Alizadeh S, Daneshjoo A, et al. Acute Effects of Various Stretching Techniques on Range of Motion: A Systematic Review with Meta-Analysis. Sports Med Open. 2023;9:107.
  • Warneke K, et al. Effects of Chronic Static Stretching on Maximal Strength and Muscle Hypertrophy: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis with Meta-Regression. Sports Med Open. 2024.
  • Coleman M, Burke R, et al. Gaining more from doing less? The effects of a one-week deload period during supervised resistance training on muscular adaptations. PeerJ. 2024;12:e16777. (39 trained adults; complete one-week cessation at week 5 of 9; equal hypertrophy, slightly better lower-body strength with continuous training.)
  • Pelland JC, Remmert JF, Robinson Z, Hinson SR, Zourdos MC. The Resistance Training Dose-Response: Meta-Regressions Exploring the Effects of Weekly Volume and Frequency on Muscle Hypertrophy and Strength Gain. SportRxiv preprint, 2024 (peer-reviewed version 2025). Frequency effects small once weekly volume is matched.
  • Spina A. Functional Range Conditioning (FRC), Functional Anatomy Seminars: origin of CARs (controlled articular rotations) and PAILs/RAILs (progressive and regressive angular isometric loading). Procedural numbers (2-minute passive hold, 10–20 s ramp, 5–15 s holds, 1–3 rounds, 2–3 maximal sessions per joint per week, 24–36 h soreness signal) taken from FRC-certified practitioner write-ups (Markow Training Systems, 2021–2022; Move With Purpose). FRC's own curriculum is seminar-based and not published as a paper.
  • Starrett K, Starrett J. Built to Move: The Ten Essential Habits to Help You Move Freely and Live Fully. Knopf, 2023. The Ready State.
  • Nippard J. Programme documents (Fundamentals Hypertrophy Program, Powerbuilding Phase 2.0): general warm-up of 5–10 min at ~100–120 bpm (100–135 in some), 2–3 min foam rolling on tight areas, dynamic drills, ramp-up sets. The Essentials Program (jeffnippard.com): 2–5 day options, lower-volume higher-intensity rationale. The "over 30 / 3 days / 96-hour recovery" formulation was found only in third-party summaries and is treated as paraphrase.
  • "Science Based Movers": no channel by that exact name located; not cited.
  • Bandy WD, Irion JM, Briggler M. The effect of time and frequency of static stretching on flexibility of the hamstring muscles. Phys Ther. 1997;77(10):1090–1096. (30 s as effective as 60 s; one rep per day as effective as three; 5 days/week for 6 weeks.)
  • Page P. Current concepts in muscle stretching for exercise and rehabilitation. Int J Sports Phys Ther. 2012;7(1):109–119. (15–30 s holds, 2–4 reps per position for the general population; PNF contract-relax reviewed.)
  • Kurz T. Stretching Scientifically: A Guide to Flexibility Training. 4th ed. Stadion, 2003. Cited as background physiology and for the PNF rationale; Soviet-era source base, thin on practical programming.
  • Brzycki M. Strength testing: predicting a one-rep max from reps-to-fatigue. J Phys Educ Recreat Dance. 1993;64(1):88–90. (1RM = weight × 36 ÷ (37 − reps); accuracy degrades above about 10 reps.)
  • Beighton P, Solomon L, Soskolne CL. Articular mobility in an African population. Ann Rheum Dis. 1973;32(5):413–418. Juul-Kristensen B, et al. Measurement properties of clinical assessment methods for classifying generalized joint hypermobility: a systematic review. Am J Med Genet C. 2017;175(1):116–147. (Nine-point score; adult cut-offs 4–5.)
  • Harvey D. Assessment of the flexibility of elite athletes using the modified Thomas test. Br J Sports Med. 1998;32(1):68–70. Apley's scratch test and the seated trunk-rotation test: standard clinical screens described in Magee DJ, Orthopedic Physical Assessment, 6th ed., Elsevier, 2014; home versions above are simplified for a tape measure and a wall.
  • Araújo CGS. Flexitest: An Innovative Flexibility Assessment Method. Human Kinetics, 2004. (The 20-movement, 0–4 per movement, 0–80 Flexindex battery; requires a trained assessor.)
  • PAILs/RAILs evidence status: no controlled trial isolating the branded protocol was located; a pro-FRC practitioner source (Markow Training Systems write-up) states the same. The adjacent eccentric/end-range loading literature cited is Warneke 2024 above.
  • Zhan Zhuang / standing practice: no controlled trial of standing posture practice as such was located; general qigong reviews are secondary and heterogeneous. Not cited as support; named as a future consideration only.
